</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

I've uploaded a python code repository in the directory dask__dask__2021.05. Consider the following issue description:

<issue_description>
`DataFrame.map_partitions` fails when passing in auxiliary Dask collections on distributed only
**Minimal Complete Verifiable Example**:

```python
import dask
import dask.dataframe as dd
import dask.array as da

df = dask.datasets.timeseries(freq="1d")
arr = da.ones((8, 9), chunks=2)

mapped = df.map_partitions(lambda df, arr: df.assign(x=df.x + arr.mean()), arr)

mapped.compute(scheduler="threads")  # works fine

import distributed
with distributed.Client() as client:
    mapped.compute()

---------------------------------------------------------------------------
AttributeError                            Traceback (most recent call last)
...
AttributeError: 'str' object has no attribute 'mean'
```
If we make the mapper function print out `arr` with something like:
```python
def mapper(df, arr):
    print(repr(arr))
    return df.assign(x=df.x + arr.mean())
```
we see that what's passed in for `arr` is `'finalize-bca1def2-0025-456e-a35c-00b59d569556'`—the name of the key that should be getting passed in, not its actual value.

<details><summary>Some findings from debugging</summary>

I've been poking at this for a bit, but haven't been able to figure out what the root problem is yet, so I'm just leaving all these notes here in case they're helpful.

It feels like something is going wrong in Blockwise pack/unpack. But I'm also not entirely sure the Blockwise layer is reasonably constructed to begin with either. Here are a few suspicious things:

```python
def get_key(mapping, prefix: str):
    for key, v in mapping.items():
        if key.startswith(prefix):
            return v
    raise KeyError(prefix)

bw = get_key(mapped.dask.layers, "lambda-")
bw.indices
# (('make-timeseries-caa15528836dd6a39af7b7f7cdf00c52', ('.0',)),
#  ('finalize-6504dc1d-f43a-40ef-8f35-442977d9353e', None),
#  ('finalize-6504dc1d-f43a-40ef-8f35-442977d9353e', None),
#  ('make-timeseries-caa15528836dd6a39af7b7f7cdf00c52', None))
```
Why does the Blockwise layer appear to have 4 inputs? But the `finalize-` is duplicated? And the `make-timeseries` is given as both a thing with indices (`('.0',)`) and a literal (`None`)?

Even weirder: when we pack the layer, the `indices` change:
```python
>>> packed = mapped.dask.__dask_distributed_pack__(None, {})
>>> packed["layers"][-1]
{'__module__': 'dask.blockwise',
 '__name__': 'Blockwise',
 'state': {'output': 'lambda-a76ecb36114baf96c010d10066849013',
```

**Problem Summary**: The `DataFrame.map_partitions` method fails when passing auxiliary Dask collections (like arrays) when using the distributed scheduler. The function receives string key names instead of actual computed values.

**Key Technical Details**:
- Error: `AttributeError: 'str' object has no attribute 'mean'`
- Method: `DataFrame.map_partitions`
- Variables: `df` (dataframe), `arr` (dask array)
- Files/Layers involved: Blockwise layers, distributed packing/unpacking
- String being passed: `'finalize-bca1def2-0025-456e-a35c-00b59d569556'` (should be array value)

**Steps to Reproduce**:
1. Create a Dask dataframe using `dask.datasets.timeseries()`
2. Create a Dask array using `da.ones()`  
3. Call `df.map_partitions(lambda df, arr: df.assign(x=df.x + arr.mean()), arr)`
4. Works with `scheduler="threads"` but fails with distributed Client

**Best Practices for Fix**:
- Minimal changes to core functionality
- Ensure compatibility with both local and distributed schedulers
- Preserve existing API behavior
- Test edge cases with different collection types

   596	
   597	        Note that the index and divisions are assumed to remain unchanged.
   598	
   599	        Parameters
   600	        ----------
   601	        func : function
   602	            Function applied to each partition.
   603	        args, kwargs :
   604	            Arguments and keywords to pass to the function. The partition will
   605	            be the first argument, and these will be passed *after*. Arguments
   606	            and keywords may contain ``Scalar``, ``Delayed``, ``partition_info``
   607	            or regular python objects. DataFrame-like args (both dask and
   608	            pandas) will be repartitioned to align (if necessary) before
   609	            applying the function.
   610	        $META
   611	
   612	        Examples
   613	        --------
   614	        Given a DataFrame, Series, or Index, such as:
   615	
   616	        >>> import pandas as pd
   617	        >>> import dask.dataframe as dd
   618	        >>> df = pd.DataFrame({'x': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5],
   619	        ...                    'y': [1., 2., 3., 4., 5.]})
   620	        >>> ddf = dd.from_pandas(df, npartitions=2)
   621	
   622	        One can use ``map_partitions`` to apply a function on each partition.
   623	        Extra arguments and keywords can optionally be provided, and will be
   624	        passed to the function after the partition.
   625	
   626	        Here we apply a function with arguments and keywords to a DataFrame,
   627	        resulting in a Series:
   628	
   629	        >>> def myadd(df, a, b=1):
   630	        ...     return df.x + df.y + a + b
   631	        >>> res = ddf.map_partitions(myadd, 1, b=2)
   632	        >>> res.dtype
   633	        dtype('float64')
   634	
   635	        By default, dask tries to infer the output metadata by running your
   636	        provided function on some fake data. This works well in many cases, but
   637	        can sometimes be expensive, or even fail. To avoid this, you can
   638	        manually specify the output metadata with the ``meta`` keyword. This
   639	        can be specified in many forms, for more information see
   640	        ``dask.dataframe.utils.make_meta``.
   641	
   642	        Here we specify the output is a Series with no name, and dtype
   643	        ``float64``:
   644	
   645	        >>> res = ddf.map_partitions(myadd, 1, b=2, meta=(None, 'f8'))
   646	
   647	        Here we map a function that takes in a DataFrame, and returns a
   648	        DataFrame with a new column:
   649	
   650	        >>> res = ddf.map_partitions(lambda df: df.assign(z=df.x * df.y))

  6793	def partitionwise_graph(func, name, *args, **kwargs):
  6794	    """
  6795	    Apply a function partition-wise across arguments to create layer of a graph
  6796	
  6797	    This applies a function, ``func``, in an embarrassingly parallel fashion
  6798	    across partitions/chunks in the provided arguments.  It handles Dataframes,
  6799	    Arrays, and scalars smoothly, and relies on the ``blockwise`` machinery
  6800	    to provide a nicely symbolic graph.
  6801	
  6802	    It is most commonly used in other graph-building functions to create the
  6803	    appropriate layer of the resulting dataframe.
  6804	
  6805	    Parameters
  6806	    ----------
  6807	    func: callable
  6808	    name: str
  6809	        descriptive name for the operation
  6810	    *args:
  6811	    **kwargs:
  6812	
  6813	    Returns
  6814	    -------
  6815	    out: Blockwise graph
  6816	
  6817	    Examples
  6818	    --------
  6819	    >>> subgraph = partitionwise_graph(function, x, y, z=123)  # doctest: +SKIP
  6820	    >>> layer = partitionwise_graph(function, df, x, z=123)  # doctest: +SKIP
  6821	    >>> graph = HighLevelGraph.from_collections(name, layer, dependencies=[df, x])  # doctest: +SKIP
  6822	    >>> result = new_dd_object(graph, name, metadata, df.divisions)  # doctest: +SKIP
  6823	
  6824	    See Also
  6825	    --------
  6826	    map_partitions
  6827	    """
  6828	    pairs = []
  6829	    numblocks = {}
  6830	    for arg in args:
  6831	        if isinstance(arg, _Frame):
  6832	            pairs.extend([arg._name, "i"])
  6833	            numblocks[arg._name] = (arg.npartitions,)
  6834	        elif isinstance(arg, Scalar):
  6835	            pairs.extend([arg._name, "i"])
  6836	            numblocks[arg._name] = (1,)
  6837	        elif isinstance(arg, Array):
  6838	            if arg.ndim == 1:
  6839	                pairs.extend([arg.name, "i"])
  6840	            elif arg.ndim == 0:
  6841	                pairs.extend([arg.name, ""])
  6842	            elif arg.ndim == 2:
  6843	                pairs.extend([arg.name, "ij"])
  6844	            else:
  6845	                raise ValueError("Can't add multi-dimensional array to dataframes")
  6846	            numblocks[arg._name] = arg.numblocks
  6847	        else:
  6848	            pairs.extend([arg, None])
  6849	    return blockwise(
  6850	        func, name, "i", *pairs, numblocks=numblocks, concatenate=True, **kwargs

    34	class BlockwiseDep:
    35	    """Blockwise-IO argument
    36	
    37	    This is the base class for indexable Blockwise-IO arguments.
    38	    When constructing a ``Blockwise`` Layer, one or more of the
    39	    collection tuples passed in with ``indices`` may contain a
    40	    ``BlockwiseDep`` instance (in place of a "real" collection name).
    41	    This allows a new collection to be created (via IO) within a
    42	    ``Blockwise`` layer.
    43	
    44	    All ``BlockwiseDep`` instances must define a ``numblocks``

   308	
   309	class Blockwise(Layer):
   310	    """Tensor Operation
   311	
   312	    This is a lazily constructed mapping for tensor operation graphs.
   313	    This defines a dictionary using an operation and an indexing pattern.
   314	    It is built for many operations like elementwise, transpose, tensordot, and
   315	    so on.  We choose to keep these as symbolic mappings rather than raw
   316	    dictionaries because we are able to fuse them during optimization,
   317	    sometimes resulting in much lower overhead.
   318	
   319	    Parameters
   320	    ----------
   321	    output: str
   322	        The name of the output collection.  Used in keynames
   323	    output_indices: tuple
   324	        The output indices, like ``('i', 'j', 'k')`` used to determine the
   325	        structure of the block computations
   326	    dsk: dict
   327	        A small graph to apply per-output-block.  May include keys from the
   328	        input indices.
   329	    indices: Tuple[Tuple[str, Optional[Tuple[str, ...]]], ...]
   330	        An ordered mapping from input key name, like ``'x'``
   331	        to input indices, like ``('i', 'j')``
   332	        Or includes literals, which have ``None`` for an index value.
   333	        In place of input-key names, the first tuple element may also be a
   334	        ``BlockwiseDep`` object.
   335	    numblocks: Mapping[key, Sequence[int]]
   336	        Number of blocks along each dimension for each input
   337	    concatenate: bool
   338	        Whether or not to pass contracted dimensions as a list of inputs or a
   339	        single input to the block function
   340	    new_axes: Mapping
   341	        New index dimensions that may have been created and their size,
   342	        e.g. ``{'j': 2, 'k': 3}``
   343	    output_blocks: Set[Tuple[int, ...]]
   344	        Specify a specific set of required output blocks. Since the graph
   345	        will only contain the necessary tasks to generate these outputs,
   346	        this kwarg can be used to "cull" the abstract layer (without needing
   347	        to materialize the low-level graph).
   348	    annotations: dict (optional)
   349	        Layer annotations
   350	    io_deps: Dict[str, BlockwiseDep] (optional)
   351	        Dictionary containing the mapping between "place-holder" collection
   352	        keys and ``BlockwiseDep``-based objects.
   353	        **WARNING**: This argument should only be used internally (for culling,
   354	        fusion and cloning of existing Blockwise layers). Explicit use of this
   355	        argument will be deprecated in the future.
   356	
   357	    See Also
   358	    --------
   359	    dask.blockwise.blockwise
   360	    dask.array.blockwise
   361	    """
   362	
   363	    output: str
   364	    output_indices: Tuple[str, ...]
   365	    dsk: Mapping[str, tuple]
   366	    indices: Tuple[Tuple[str, Optional[Tuple[str, ...]]], ...]
   367	    numblocks: Mapping[str, Sequence[int]]
   368	    concatenate: Optional[bool]
   369	    new_axes: Mapping[str, int]
   370	    output_blocks: Optional[Set[Tuple[int, ...]]]

   512	        # is a `BlockwiseDep`-based object.

   516	            packed_io_deps[name] = {

   525	        # will NOT be producing "nested" tasks (via `__dask_distributed_unpack__`).
   526	        #
   527	        # If `make_blockwise_graph` DOES need to produce nested tasks later on, it
   528	        # will need to call `to_serialize` on the entire task.  That will be a
   529	        # problem if the function was already pickled here. Therefore, we want to
   530	        # call `to_serialize` on the function if we know there will be nested tasks.

   532	        # We know there will be nested tasks if either:

   864	def make_blockwise_graph(
   865	    func,
   866	    output,
   867	    out_indices,
   868	    *arrind_pairs,
   869	    numblocks=None,
   870	    concatenate=None,
   871	    new_axes=None,
   872	    output_blocks=None,
   873	    dims=None,
   874	    deserializing=False,
   875	    func_future_args=None,
   876	    return_key_deps=False,
   877	    io_deps=None,
   878	    **kwargs,
   879	):
   880	    """Tensor operation
   881	
   882	    Applies a function, ``func``, across blocks from many different input
   883	    collections.  We arrange the pattern with which those blocks interact with
   884	    sets of matching indices.  E.g.::
   885	
   886	        make_blockwise_graph(func, 'z', 'i', 'x', 'i', 'y', 'i')
   887	
   888	    yield an embarrassingly parallel communication pattern and is read as
   889	
   890	        $$ z_i = func(x_i, y_i) $$
   891	
   892	    More complex patterns may emerge, including multiple indices::
   893	
   894	        make_blockwise_graph(func, 'z', 'ij', 'x', 'ij', 'y', 'ji')
   895	
   896	        $$ z_{ij} = func(x_{ij}, y_{ji}) $$
   897	
   898	    Indices missing in the output but present in the inputs results in many
   899	    inputs being sent to one function (see examples).
   900	
   901	    Examples
   902	    --------
   903	    Simple embarrassing map operation
   904	
   905	    >>> inc = lambda x: x + 1
   906	    >>> make_blockwise_graph(inc, 'z', 'ij', 'x', 'ij', numblocks={'x': (2, 2)})  # doctest: +SKIP
   907	    {('z', 0, 0): (inc, ('x', 0, 0)),
   908	     ('z', 0, 1): (inc, ('x', 0, 1)),
   909	     ('z', 1, 0): (inc, ('x', 1, 0)),
   910	     ('z', 1, 1): (inc, ('x', 1, 1))}
   911	
   912	    Simple operation on two datasets
   913	
   914	    >>> add = lambda x, y: x + y
   915	    >>> make_blockwise_graph(add, 'z', 'ij', 'x', 'ij', 'y', 'ij', numblocks={'x': (2, 2),
   916	    ...                                                      'y': (2, 2)})  # doctest: +SKIP
   917	    {('z', 0, 0): (add, ('x', 0, 0), ('y', 0, 0)),
   918	     ('z', 0, 1): (add, ('x', 0, 1), ('y', 0, 1)),
   919	     ('z', 1, 0): (add, ('x', 1, 0), ('y', 1, 0)),
   920	     ('z', 1, 1): (add, ('x', 1, 1), ('y', 1, 1))}

In the `__dask_distributed_pack__` method in Blockwise class (lines 487-577), the packing process separates literal values (where index is None) and stores them directly in the dsk dictionary (line 503):

```python
# Embed literals in `dsk`
keys2 = []
indices2 = []
for key, (val, index) in zip(keys, self.indices):
    if index is None:  # Literal
        dsk[key] = val
    else:
        keys2.append(key)
        indices2.append((val, index))
```

However, in the `__dask_distributed_unpack__` method (lines 580-626), the indices are reconstructed but the literal values that were embedded in the dsk are not properly handled. The `make_blockwise_graph` function is called with `deserializing=True`, and when `ind is None` (literal case), it calls:

```python
if deserializing:
    args.append(stringify_collection_keys(arg))
else:
    args.append(arg)
```

But the problem is that the literal values that were embedded in the `dsk` during packing are not being retrieved and passed correctly during unpacking. They're being treated as stringified keys instead of actual values.

Looking at the debug output in the issue description, I can see that in the packed version, the indices only contain:
`'indices': ['make-timeseries-caa15528836dd6a39af7b7f7cdf00c52', ('.0',)]`

But originally there were more entries including the literal values. Those literals got lost in the packing process and are not being restored properly during unpacking.

The issue is that when `make_blockwise_graph` is called with `deserializing=True`, it needs to handle the case where literal values were embedded in the SubgraphCallable's dsk, but it's not doing that correctly.

Let me look at how the SubgraphCallable is being constructed and used.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me look at how the SubgraphCallable is constructed in the packing process:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/dask/blockwise.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[500, 520]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/dask/blockwise.py:

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>I see! The literals are stored in the `dsk` dictionary and then a `SubgraphCallable` is created with that dsk. The SubgraphCallable contains the literal values. But when the SubgraphCallable is unpacked later, those literal values should be accessible.

Let me look at what happens in the unpacking process. The issue mentions that the unpacked task shows the subgraph callable with extra keys like `_1`, `_2`, `_3` that contain string key names instead of actual values.

# Define mapper function that shows what's being passed
def mapper(df, arr):
    print(f"Received arr type: {type(arr)}, value: {repr(arr)}")
    if isinstance(arr, str):
        raise AttributeError(f"'{arr}' object has no attribute 'mean'")
    return df.assign(x=df.x + arr.mean())

print("\nTesting with threads scheduler (should work)...")
try:
    mapped = df.map_partitions(mapper, arr)
    result = mapped.compute(scheduler="threads")
    print("✓ Threads scheduler works!")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"✗ Threads scheduler failed: {e}")

print("\nTesting with distributed scheduler (should fail)...")

    print(f"✗ Distributed scheduler failed: {e}")

"""
Simple reproduction script for the DataFrame.map_partitions issue with distributed scheduler.
"""

import dask
import dask.dataframe as dd
import dask.array as da

print("Testing DataFrame.map_partitions with auxiliary Dask collections")

# Create test data
print("Creating test data...")
df = dask.datasets.timeseries(freq="1d")
arr = da.ones((8, 9), chunks=2)

print(f"DataFrame partitions: {df.npartitions}")
print(f"Array chunks: {arr.npartitions}")

# Simple mapper function that shows what's being passed
def mapper(df, arr):
    print(f"Received arr type: {type(arr)}, repr: {repr(arr)}")
    if isinstance(arr, str):
        raise AttributeError(f"'{arr}' object has no attribute 'mean'")
    return df.assign(x=df.x + arr.mean())

print("\nTesting with threads scheduler (should work)...")
try:
    mapped = df.map_partitions(mapper, arr)
    result = mapped.compute(scheduler="threads")
    print("✓ Threads scheduler works!")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"✗ Threads scheduler failed: {e}")

print("\nTesting with distributed scheduler (should fail)...")
try:
    import distributed
    with distributed.Client(processes=False, silence_logs=False) as client:
        mapped = df.map_partitions(mapper, arr)
        result = mapped.compute()
        print("✓ Distributed scheduler works!")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"✗ Distributed scheduler failed: {e}")
    import traceback
    traceback.print_exc()

print("\nDone.")</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>File created successfully at: /workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/simple_repro.py</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>execute_bash<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>cd /workspace/dask__dask__2021.05 && python simple_repro.py</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Testing DataFrame.map_partitions with auxiliary Dask collections

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! I've reproduced the issue. The problem is that when using the distributed scheduler, the arguments are being serialized incorrectly. The error `TypeError('cannot unpack non-iterable Serialize object')` indicates that a `Serialize` object is being passed where a tuple/list was expected.

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Based on my analysis of the issue and the reproduction script, here's what I understand:

1. **Problem**: When `DataFrame.map_partitions` is used with auxiliary Dask collections (like arrays) and the distributed scheduler, the arguments are not being properly deserialized. Instead of receiving the actual array values, the function receives `Serialize` objects that can't be unpacked.

2. **Location**: The issue is in the `__dask_distributed_pack__` and `__dask_distributed_unpack__` methods of the `Blockwise` class in `/workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/dask/blockwise.py`.

3. **Root Cause**: Looking at the issue description and the debug output, the problem is that literal values (auxiliary Dask collections) are being embedded in the SubgraphCallable's dsk during packing, but they're not being properly handled during unpacking.

In the packing process (lines 500-508):
```python
# Embed literals in `dsk`
keys2 = []
indices2 = []
for key, (val, index) in zip(keys, self.indices):
    if index is None:  # Literal
        dsk[key] = val
    else:
        keys2.append(key)
        indices2.append((val, index))

dsk = (SubgraphCallable(dsk, self.output, tuple(keys2)),)
```

The literal values are stored directly in the `dsk` dictionary and then wrapped in a SubgraphCallable. However, during unpacking, these literals are not being properly extracted and passed as arguments.

4. **How the test reproduces the issue**: My test creates a DataFrame and an Array, then uses `map_partitions` to pass the array as an argument. With the threads scheduler, everything works fine because no serialization is involved. With the distributed scheduler, the arguments get serialized/deserialized incorrectly.

5. **Best practices for the fix**:
   - Make minimal changes to preserve existing functionality
   - Ensure the fix works for both local and distributed schedulers
   - Don't break existing behavior for cases that currently work
   - Focus on the specific case where auxiliary Dask collections are passed as arguments

6. **How to fix the problem**: 
   The issue is that when `make_blockwise_graph` is called with `deserializing=True` in the `__dask_distributed_unpack__` method, it's not properly handling the case where literal values were embedded in the SubgraphCallable's dsk. These literals need to be extracted and passed as additional arguments.

Looking more closely at the debug output in the issue description, I can see that in the packed version, the indices only contain:
`'indices': ['make-timeseries-caa15528836dd6a39af7b7f7cdf00c52', ('.0',)]`

But the original had more entries including the literal values. The literals got embedded in the SubgraphCallable's dsk but are not being recovered properly.

The fix should ensure that when the SubgraphCallable is deserialized, any literal values that were embedded in its dsk are properly extracted and passed as arguments to the function.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me examine the debug output from the issue more carefully and look at how the SubgraphCallable is being handled:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/debug_issue.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3

df = dask.datasets.timeseries(freq="1d")

        lambda_layer_key = key
        break

    # Manually create the SubgraphCallable like in __dask_distributed_pack__
    from dask.blockwise import blockwise_token
    from dask.optimization import SubgraphCallable
    from dask.core import flatten
    
    keys = tuple(map(blockwise_token, range(len(bw_layer.indices))))
    dsk = dict(bw_layer.dsk)  # Copy the original dsk

    indices2 = []

        print(f"Processing key={key}, val={val}, index={index}")
        if index is None:  # Literal
            print(f"  -> Literal: embedding {val} in dsk[{key}]")
            dsk[key] = val
        else:
            print(f"  -> Non-literal: keeping {val} with index {index}")
            keys2.append(key)
            indices2.append((val, index))

Received arr type: <class 'str'>

The auxiliary dask array is being passed as a string key name instead of the actual array value. This is exactly what the issue describes.

Now I can focus on fixing this specific problem. The issue is that during distributed serialization/deserialization, the literal values (auxiliary dask collections) are being passed as their key names instead of their actual computed values.

Let me think about where this conversion should happen. The literals are embedded in the SubgraphCallable's dsk during packing, but during unpacking they should be resolved to actual values.

Looking at the debug output from the issue:
> The subgraph_callable only takes one input, yet has these extra `_1`, `_2`, `_3` keys in it. I imagine that's where the `'finalize-'` string that's getting passed into our mapper function and causing the error is coming from.

So the embedded literals in the SubgraphCallable's dsk contain string key names, and somehow these are ending up as arguments to the mapper function.

But wait, in my current setup, the SubgraphCallable only takes one input (the dataframe), but the mapper function expects two arguments (dataframe and array). How is this supposed to work?

Let me check the SubgraphCallable's structure:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/dask__dask__2021.05/examine_subgraph_simple.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Examine the SubgraphCallable structure for simple case.
"""

import pandas as pd
import dask.dataframe as dd
import dask.array as da

# Create simple test data

    'x': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6],

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>There it is! Line 966: `core.get(self.dsk, self.outkey, dict(zip(self.inkeys, args)))`

The SubgraphCallable takes the arguments passed to it and substitutes them into its internal dsk using `dict(zip(self.inkeys, args))`. 

So if `self.inkeys = ['_0', '_1']` and `args = (dataframe_partition, 'finalize-...')`, then it creates a substitution dict `{'_0': dataframe_partition, '_1': 'finalize-...'}`.

But the problem is that `'_1'` is a string key name, not the actual computed value. The dsk internally has references to `'_1'` that should be resolved.

However, the `core.get` function should resolve key names in the dsk to actual values. So if the dsk contains a task that references `'_1'`, `core.get` should compute the value of `'_1'` from the substitution dict.

But in our case, the literal is embedded directly in the dsk as `'_1': 'finalize-...'`. This means the literal VALUE is the string `'finalize-...'`, not a reference to be resolved.

This suggests that the issue is in the packing process - the literal should be embedded as a proper task that can be resolved, not as a raw string.
